    The shell's word for SIGKILL, exit code 137: in a container, usually the kernel OOM killer, with no Go error at all. GOMEMLIMIT is soft and does not prevent it. Check dmesg -T | grep -i 'out of memory' and docker inspect for OOMKilled.

    Prometheus and Alertmanager

    Prometheus usually breaks in four ways: a target stops answering, a rule or a config reload fails, the series count grows until memory runs out, or an alert fires and no notification arrives. Check the targets first, then the rules, then Alertmanager. Commands use http://HOST:9090 for Prometheus and http://HOST:9093 for Alertmanager (the defaults), and run from any machine that can reach them. promtool and amtool ship in the release tarballs and images; run promtool check on the host that holds the files. Queries run with promtool query instant, which sends no credentials: add --http.config.file=FILE when the server needs a login. JSON output is read with jq. Output is from Prometheus 3.16 and Alertmanager 0.35.

    Prometheus: look first

    curl -s http://HOST:9090/-/healthy; curl -s http://HOST:9090/-/ready

    Prometheus Server is Healthy. means the process is up; Prometheus Server is Ready. means it serves queries. A non-200 from /-/ready right after a start is a server still loading: the log shows Replaying WAL, this may take a while.

    curl -s http://HOST:9090/api/v1/status/runtimeinfo

    Read reloadConfigSuccess (false means the last reload failed and the old config still runs), corruptionCount (WAL corruption repaired since start), storageRetention and goroutineCount. The same call on /api/v1/status/buildinfo gives the version.

    curl -s http://HOST:9090/api/v1/targets?state=active | jq -r '.data.activeTargets[] | select(.health != "up") | [.scrapePool, .scrapeUrl, .lastError] | @tsv'

    One line per target that is not up: the job, the URL it scrapes and why it failed. The lastError text names the cause: see the errors group at the end of this section.

    promtool query instant http://HOST:9090 'up == 0'

    Every target that failed its last scrape, with its job and instance labels. Empty output is healthy. Use count by (job) (up == 0) for a count per job.

    promtool query instant http://HOST:9090 'absent(up{job="JOB"})'

    Returns 1 when no up series exists for the job at all: the job was removed from the config, or service discovery found nothing. A job that is down still has up == 0, so it does not match here.

    promtool check config FILE; promtool check rules FILE

    Parses the config and every rule file it names, and prints SUCCESS for each. Run it before every reload. Typical failures: a YAML error with its line number, an unknown duration unit, a missing rule file.

    curl -s -X POST http://HOST:9090/-/reload

    Reloads the config and rule files without a restart. An empty reply with HTTP 200 is success; HTTP 500 means the new config was rejected and the old one keeps running (the log has Error reloading config). HTTP 403 Lifecycle API is not enabled. means the server runs without --web.enable-lifecycle: send kill -HUP PID instead.

    Changes state

    curl -s http://HOST:9090/api/v1/rules | jq -r '.data.groups[].rules[] | select(.health != "ok") | [.name, .lastError] | @tsv'

    Rules whose last evaluation failed, with the error. An empty result is healthy. A rule that errors never fires and never records, so a broken alert looks the same as a quiet one.

    curl -s http://HOST:9090/api/v1/alerts | jq -r '.data.alerts[] | [.labels.alertname, .state, .activeAt] | @tsv'

    Alerts Prometheus holds now. pending is still inside the rule's for time; firing is sent to Alertmanager. An alert missing here never reached Alertmanager, so look at the rule. One that fires here but is not in Alertmanager points to the notifier.

    journalctl -u APP --since "1 hour ago" | grep -E 'level=(WARN|ERROR)'

    Prometheus 3 logs key=value lines with an upper-case level. Most scrape failures are not logged at the default level: read them from the targets API. The warnings and errors here are rule failures, WAL repairs, remote write failures and notifier errors.

    Prometheus: queries for a host or service

    promtool query instant http://HOST:9090 '1 - avg by (instance) (rate(node_cpu_seconds_total{mode="idle"}[5m]))'

    CPU busy fraction per host over five minutes, from node_exporter: 1 is every core busy. Above about 0.9 for several minutes the host is saturated. For one hot process, look at process_cpu_seconds_total instead.

    promtool query instant http://HOST:9090 '1 - node_memory_MemAvailable_bytes / node_memory_MemTotal_bytes'

    Fraction of memory in use, counting cache as free the way the kernel does. Above 0.9 for a long time means real pressure. Compare with process_resident_memory_bytes to find the process.

    promtool query instant http://HOST:9090 'predict_linear(node_filesystem_avail_bytes{fstype!~"tmpfs|overlay"}[6h], 24*3600) < 0'

    Filesystems that the last six hours of trend will fill within a day. Each line is a mount that runs out. Empty output is healthy. Use a shorter window such as [1h] and 4*3600 for a fast leak.

    promtool query instant http://HOST:9090 'sum(rate(http_requests_total{job="JOB",code=~"5.."}[5m])) / sum(rate(http_requests_total{job="JOB"}[5m]))'

    Share of requests that returned a 5xx. Replace http_requests_total and code with the names your service exports. No output means the metric or the label does not exist for that job. It can also mean no 5xx since the last restart: many client libraries create the 5xx series on the first error.

    Prometheus: cardinality and memory

    curl -s 'http://HOST:9090/api/v1/status/tsdb?limit=10' | jq '.data.seriesCountByMetricName'

    The ten metric names with the most series in the head block, plus headStats.numSeries in the full reply. The first names are the cause of a memory climb: look for a label that holds an ID, a URL or a user. Other keys: labelValueCountByLabelName, seriesCountByLabelValuePair.

    promtool query instant http://HOST:9090 'topk(10, count by (__name__) ({__name__=~".+"}))'

    The same ranking from a query. It reads every series, so on a large server it can hit the sample limit or time out: use the TSDB status call first. It only sees series that are stored: a target that fails with sample limit exceeded has no series here.

    Prometheus: storage, ingestion and notifications

    promtool query instant http://HOST:9090 'increase(prometheus_tsdb_compactions_failed_total[1h]) > 0 or increase(prometheus_tsdb_wal_corruptions_total[1h]) > 0 or increase(prometheus_tsdb_reloads_failures_total[1h]) > 0'

    Any output means the TSDB had a compaction failure, a WAL corruption or a block reload failure in the last hour. Read the log for the first error. Compaction failures usually mean a full disk or bad permissions on the data directory.

    promtool query instant http://HOST:9090 'increase(prometheus_target_scrapes_sample_out_of_order_total[15m]) > 0 or increase(prometheus_target_scrapes_sample_duplicate_timestamp_total[15m]) > 0 or increase(prometheus_target_scrapes_sample_out_of_bounds_total[15m]) > 0'

    Samples the TSDB refused: out of order, a repeated timestamp with a different value, or a timestamp outside the head window. These counters carry no target label, so the target comes from the WARN log line (target=). Two targets exposing the same series, or an exporter that sets its own timestamps, cause these.

    promtool query instant http://HOST:9090 'rate(prometheus_notifications_errors_total[5m]) > 0 or increase(prometheus_notifications_dropped_total[5m]) > 0'

    Alerts that failed to reach an Alertmanager, by alertmanager label. Check that address first. prometheus_notifications_alertmanagers_discovered shows how many Alertmanagers Prometheus knows; curl -s http://HOST:9090/api/v1/alertmanagers lists their URLs.

    promtool query instant http://HOST:9090 'prometheus_remote_storage_highest_timestamp_in_seconds - ignoring(remote_name, url) group_right(instance) prometheus_remote_storage_queue_highest_sent_timestamp_seconds'

    Remote write lag in seconds for each endpoint. A few seconds is normal; a value that grows is a backlog. A value near the current Unix time means nothing has been sent yet. Pair it with the failure rate below.

    promtool query instant http://HOST:9090 'rate(prometheus_remote_storage_samples_failed_total[5m]) > 0 or rate(prometheus_remote_storage_samples_retried_total[5m]) > 0'

    Failed samples are dropped for good (a 4xx reply); retried samples are being resent (a 5xx or a network error). The url label names the endpoint. The log says why: see the errors group. prometheus_remote_storage_shards at shards_max means the queue cannot keep up.

    Alertmanager: look first

    amtool --alertmanager.url=http://HOST:9093 alert query -o extended

    Active alerts Alertmanager holds now, with all labels and the start and end time. Silenced and inhibited alerts are hidden by default, so an alert missing here may be suppressed rather than lost: run the next command before you decide it never arrived. If it is in neither list, check the notifier.

    amtool --alertmanager.url=http://HOST:9093 alert query --inhibited; amtool --alertmanager.url=http://HOST:9093 alert query --silenced

    The suppressed alerts, one list for each reason, with State suppressed. An alert in the first list is held back by an inhibit rule because a more severe one fires; one in the second matches a silence. This is the usual answer to "the alert fired and nobody was told".

    curl -s http://HOST:9093/metrics | grep -E '^alertmanager_cluster_(members|failed_peers|health_score)'

    alertmanager_cluster_members should equal the number of Alertmanagers in the HA set. A lower number on each node means they do not gossip, and each node sends its own notifications, so every alert pages more than once. amtool cluster show prints Cluster Status: ready, or disabled when clustering is off. Prometheus must send to every instance.

    amtool --alertmanager.url=http://HOST:9093 silence query

    Active silences with their ID, matchers, end time, author and comment. Add --expired to see the ones that ended. A silence with broad matchers or no end that nobody remembers is a common cause of missed pages.

    amtool --alertmanager.url=http://HOST:9093 silence add alertname=TargetDown --duration=2h --comment="reason, ticket" --author="your name"

    Mutes alerts that match every matcher, for the duration, and prints the new silence ID. Alerts still fire and show as suppressed. Never silence Watchdog or any dead-man alert. Match on more than alertname when you can.

    Changes state

    amtool --alertmanager.url=http://HOST:9093 silence expire ID

    Ends a silence now and prints nothing on success. The alerts it muted notify again at the next group interval. silence query -q prints only the IDs, to feed this command.

    Changes state

    amtool check-config FILE

    Checks the Alertmanager config and counts routes, inhibit rules, receivers and templates. A broken file ends with FAILED: and the cause, such as undefined receiver "oncall" used in route.

    amtool config routes show --config.file=FILE; amtool config routes test --config.file=FILE severity=page alertname=TargetDown

    The first command prints the routing tree; the second prints the receiver an alert with those labels would reach. Add --verify.receivers=RECEIVER to make the exit code 1 when the receiver differs. Use it to find a route that sends alerts to the wrong place.

    curl -s -X POST http://HOST:9093/-/reload

    Reloads the config. HTTP 500 means the file was rejected and the old config stays active; the log has Loading configuration file failed. alertmanager_config_last_reload_successful is 0 after a failed reload. Run amtool check-config first.

    Changes state

    curl -s http://HOST:9093/metrics | grep -E '^alertmanager_(notifications_failed_total|notification_requests_failed_total)' | grep -v ' 0$'

    Notification failures per integration. Notification failures per integration. notifications_failed_total carries a reason: serverError is a 5xx from the receiver, clientError a 4xx, rateLimited a 429, contextDeadlineExceeded a timeout. A refused connection is counted as other. No output means no failures since the last start.

    Prometheus and Alertmanager errors

    Get "http://192.0.2.10:9100/metrics": dial tcp 192.0.2.10:9100: connect: connection refused Get "http://app.example.net:9100/metrics": dial tcp: lookup app.example.net on 127.0.0.53:53: no such host

    The first: nothing listens on that port, or a firewall rejects it; test with curl from the Prometheus host. The second: the name does not resolve from the Prometheus host, so check the resolver it uses (shown after on) and the service discovery that produced the name.

    Get "http://192.0.2.10:9100/metrics": context deadline exceeded

    The target accepted the connection but did not finish the reply inside scrape_timeout. The scrape_duration_seconds metric for the target shows how close it runs to the limit. Fix a slow exporter first; raise scrape_timeout only when the target is honestly big.

    server returned HTTP status 401 Unauthorized server returned HTTP status 404 Not Found

    The target answers but refuses or lacks the path. A 401 needs authorization or basic_auth in the scrape config. A 404 is a wrong metrics_path or a port that serves something else. Test the URL with curl -i.

    received unsupported Content-Type "text/html" and no fallback_scrape_protocol specified for target

    The endpoint returned a page that is not a metrics format, often a login page, a proxy error or the wrong path. Prometheus 3 refuses a missing or unknown Content-Type. Fix the endpoint; set fallback_scrape_protocol (for example PrometheusText0.0.4) only when it really serves that format.

    sample limit exceeded

    The target's lastError when a job sets sample_limit and one scrape returned more samples. The whole scrape is rejected and its series go stale, so the target is down and its series are not in the TSDB. Compare scrape_samples_scraped{job="JOB"} with the limit, and count names in the target's own output with curl -s URL | cut -d'{' -f1 | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn | head.

    level=WARN source=group.go:548 msg="Evaluating rule failed" component="rule manager" file=/etc/prometheus/rules.yml group=demo name=bad:many err="found duplicate series for the match group {} on the left hand-side of the operation: [...];many-to-many matching not allowed: matching labels must be unique on one side"

    A rule's expression matched more than one series on a side where it needs one. Run the expression in a query to see the series, then add labels to on(...) or ignoring(...), or aggregate first. The rule shows health: err in the rules API.

    level=WARN msg="Error on ingesting out-of-order samples" component="scrape manager" scrape_pool=JOB target=http://192.0.2.10:9100/metrics num_dropped=1 level=WARN msg="Error on ingesting samples with different value but same timestamp" component="scrape manager" scrape_pool=JOB target=http://192.0.2.10:9100/metrics num_dropped=1

    The target exposes a sample with its own timestamp that is older than the last stored one, or repeats a timestamp with a new value. The sample is dropped. Remove the explicit timestamps in the exporter, or find two targets that produce the same series.

    level=WARN source=queue_manager.go:2408 msg="Failed to send batch, retrying" component=remote remote_name=rwdest url=http://192.0.2.10:9201/write err="server returned HTTP status 500 Internal Server Error: boom" level=ERROR source=queue_manager.go:2042 msg="non-recoverable error" component=remote remote_name=rwdest url=http://192.0.2.10:9201/write failedSampleCount=1483 err="server returned HTTP status 400 Bad Request: bad samples"

    A 5xx or network error is retried while the queue holds the samples. Prometheus truncates the WAL on schedule (about every two hours), so an outage longer than that loses the oldest samples; the disk does not fill. A 4xx is non-recoverable: those samples are dropped. Read the receiver's own log for the cause of the 400.

    level=WARN source=db.go:1241 msg="Encountered WAL read error, attempting repair" component=tsdb err="read records: corruption in segment /prometheus/wal/00000000 at 18066: unexpected checksum 5e40df53, expected c1927e54"

    The WAL has a damaged record, usually after a crash or a full disk. Prometheus then logs Successfully repaired WAL and drops the damaged segment and every later one, which loses the most recent samples. Alert on prometheus_tsdb_wal_corruptions_total.

    level=ERROR source=main.go:1678 msg="Fatal error" err="opening storage failed: lock DB directory: resource temporarily unavailable"

    Another Prometheus process holds the data directory. Find it with ps, often an old instance still running or two units sharing one path. Stop the extra one. Never delete the lock file under a live process.

    level=ERROR source=main.go:1442 msg="Error reloading config" err="couldn't load configuration (--config.file=\"/etc/prometheus/prometheus.yml\"): parsing YAML file /etc/prometheus/prometheus.yml: yaml: line 33: did not find expected '-' indicator"

    The reload was rejected and the old config still runs; reloadConfigSuccess is false. Run promtool check config FILE for the same error with its line number. Fix the file and reload again.

    level=ERROR source=scrape.go:1527 msg="Scrape commit failed" component="scrape manager" scrape_pool=JOB target=http://192.0.2.10:9100/metrics err="write to WAL: log samples: write /prometheus/wal/00000000: no space left on device"

    The data volume is full, so new samples are lost until space returns. Free space or grow the volume, and see what holds it with du -sh DATADIR/*. A size limit (--storage.tsdb.retention.size, deprecated in 3.16 in favour of the config-file setting) deletes old blocks early; keep it well below the volume size.

    level=WARN source=cluster.go:271 msg="failed to join cluster" component=cluster err="1 error occurred:\n\t* failed to resolve am2.example.net:9094: lookup am2.example.net on 127.0.0.53:53: no such host\n\n" level=ERROR source=main.go:234 msg="alertmanager exited with error" err="unable to initialize gossip mesh: create memberlist: failed to get final advertise address: failed to parse advertise address \"am1.example.net\""

    The first: this Alertmanager cannot reach its --cluster.peer, so it runs alone and retries every 10 seconds; with no gossip, every instance notifies and the receiver gets duplicates. The second: --cluster.advertise-address must be an IP address, not a name.

    level=ERROR source=sendloop.go:239 msg="Error sending alerts" component=notifier alertmanager=http://alerts.example.net:9093/api/v2/alerts count=1 err="Post \"http://alerts.example.net:9093/api/v2/alerts\": dial tcp: lookup alerts.example.net on 127.0.0.53:53: no such host"

    Prometheus cannot reach that Alertmanager, here for a DNS reason; a refused or timed-out connection prints the dial error instead. Alerts for it are dropped, counted in prometheus_notifications_dropped_total. With several Alertmanagers, one failing does not stop the others.

    level=ERROR source=coordinator.go:117 msg="Loading configuration file failed" component=configuration file=/etc/alertmanager/alertmanager.yml err="undefined receiver \"oncall\" used in route"

    Alertmanager rejected the new config and keeps the old one. A route names a receiver that is not defined, a common typo after a rename. amtool check-config FILE prints the same error before the reload.

    level=WARN source=retry_stage.go:174 msg="Notify attempt failed, will retry later" component=dispatcher receiver=oncall integration=webhook[0] attempts=1 reason=serverError err="unexpected status code 500: hook broke" level=WARN source=retry_stage.go:174 msg="Notify attempt failed, will retry later" component=dispatcher receiver=oncall integration=webhook[0] attempts=1 reason=other err="Post \"<redacted>\": dial tcp 192.0.2.30:9: connect: connection refused" level=ERROR source=dispatch.go:642 msg="Notify for alerts failed" component=dispatcher num_alerts=1 receiver=oncall err="oncall/webhook[0]: notify retry canceled after 6 attempts: unexpected status code 500: hook broke"

    The receiver rejected or did not answer the notification. The first and third lines show a 5xx, the second a refused connection (the URL is redacted in the log, so read it from the config). After the retries run out the notification is tried again at the next group_interval flush, so it is not lost until repeat_interval. Test the receiver URL with curl.

    level=DEBUG source=mute.go:117 msg="Notifications will not be sent for muted alerts" component=dispatcher alerts=[WarnDemo[6f1a394][active]] reason=inhibition

    An alert held back on purpose, with reason=inhibition. It appears only at --log.level=debug, so run amtool alert query --inhibited when the log is quiet. If the alert is not in Alertmanager at all, check the notifier on the Prometheus side.

    OpenTelemetry Collector

    The Collector sits between your apps and a backend, so most failures are a pipeline that drops data: a backend that is down or slow, a TLS mismatch, a full queue, or the memory limiter refusing data. Read the Collector's own log and its own metrics before the backend. Commands run on the Collector host. The binary is otelcol-contrib (the otelcol distribution has fewer components); the image is otel/opentelemetry-collector-contrib. Defaults used here: OTLP gRPC on 4317, OTLP HTTP on 4318, and the Collector's own metrics on 8888, which listens on localhost only unless service::telemetry::metrics::readers binds another address. The health_check (13133) and zpages (55679) extensions work only when your config enables them. Output is from Collector 0.165; metric names print without a _total suffix here, older releases add it.

    Collector: look first

    otelcol-contrib --version

    Prints otelcol-contrib version 0.165.0 or the version you run. Components are renamed and removed across releases, and a config that works on one version can fail on the next, so compare with the version the config was written for.

    otelcol-contrib validate --config=FILE

    Loads the config and checks every component and pipeline reference without starting anything. No output and exit code 0 means valid; any problem prints one Error: line and exits 1. Run it before every restart and rollout.

    otelcol-contrib components | grep 'name: otlp'

    Shows whether a receiver, processor, exporter or extension (here otlp, change it to the one you need) is compiled into this build. A component that is missing fails config load with unknown type. The core otelcol build carries only a few; the contrib build carries most.

    curl -s http://HOST:13133/

    The health_check extension: {"status":"Server available","upSince":...,"uptime":...} means the Collector runs. It does not prove data flows: an exporter can fail on every request while this stays healthy.

    ss -ltnp | grep -E ':(4317|4318|8888|13133|55679) '

    Which process listens on the Collector's ports. A missing 4317 or 4318 means the receiver did not start; check the log for bind: address already in use. On a host where Docker publishes the port, docker-proxy shows here.

    curl -si -H 'Content-Type: application/json' -d '{"resourceSpans":[{"resource":{"attributes":[{"key":"service.name","value":{"stringValue":"triage-test"}}]},"scopeSpans":[{"spans":[{"traceId":"5b8efff798038103d269b633813fc60c","spanId":"eee19b7ec3c1b174","name":"triage","kind":1,"startTimeUnixNano":"1544712660000000000","endTimeUnixNano":"1544712661000000000"}]}]}]}' http://HOST:4318/v1/traces

    Sends one test span to the OTLP HTTP receiver, and it reaches the backend. 200 with {"partialSuccess":{}} means accepted. 503 with data refused due to high memory usage is the memory limiter; 415 is a wrong Content-Type. Add a debug exporter to see the span in the log.

    Changes state

    curl -s http://HOST:8888/metrics | grep -E '^otelcol_(receiver_(accepted|refused|failed)|exporter_(sent|send_failed|enqueue_failed))'

    The Collector's own counts. receiver_refused is data a processor turned away (the memory limiter) or a full queue pushed back; exporter_send_failed is data the backend did not take. A sent counter that stops rising while accepted rises is a pipeline that drops. Labels name the exporter and the error type.

    curl -s http://HOST:8888/metrics | grep -E '^otelcol_exporter_queue_(size|capacity)'

    Queue use for each exporter. A size near capacity means the backend is slower than the input, and the next lines in the log say sending queue is full. A queue that sits at zero while data is lost points at the receiver or a processor.

    promtool query instant http://HOST:9090 'sum by (exporter) (rate({__name__=~"otelcol_exporter_send_failed_(spans|metric_points|log_records)(_total)?"}[5m])) > 0'

    For a Prometheus that scrapes the Collector: the exporters that are failing now, for any signal. Empty is healthy. The error_type label on the raw series (Unavailable, DeadlineExceeded, Unauthenticated) tells you what to look for in the log.

    curl -s http://HOST:55679/debug/pipelinez

    The zpages extension lists each pipeline with its receivers, processors and exporters. It confirms what the running Collector built, which can differ from the file you edited. /debug/servicez shows the version and runtime, and /debug/extensionz the extensions.

    journalctl -u APP --since "30 min ago" | grep -E '[[:space:]](warn|error)[[:space:]]'

    The Collector logs tab-separated lines with a lower-case level. Start at the first error after the failure began. Keep the messages that say Exporting failed; the errors group at the end of this section explains each one.

    kill -HUP PID

    Makes the Collector reload its config and restart its pipelines in place, without a new process. Data in flight and in the queues is lost, so run validate first.

    Changes state

    OpenTelemetry Collector errors

    Exporting failed. Dropping data. "error": "not retryable error: Permanent error: rpc error: code = Unimplemented desc = error exporting items, request to http://backend:4318/v1/traces responded with HTTP Status Code 404", "dropped_items": 50

    The backend returned a permanent HTTP error, so the Collector drops the batch and does not retry. A 404 is a wrong endpoint or path: otlphttp adds /v1/traces to the endpoint, so do not put it there yourself. A 401 shows as Unauthenticated: check the headers and credentials.

    Exporting failed. Will retry the request after interval. "error": "rpc error: code = Unavailable desc = no children to pick from", "interval": "744.246657ms"

    An info line, not yet a loss. gRPC found no address for the endpoint: the name does not resolve, or nothing answers. Check the endpoint with getent hosts and nc -vz. The Collector retries with growing intervals until max_elapsed_time.

    Exporting failed. Dropping data. "error": "no more retries left: rpc error: code = DeadlineExceeded desc = context deadline exceeded while waiting for connections to become ready", "dropped_items": 50

    Retries ran out: the backend accepted no connection inside the exporter timeout. For a slow or hung backend, test with nc -vz HOST PORT from the Collector host. The data is lost, so size the queue and max_elapsed_time for the outage you expect.

    Exporting failed. Will retry the request after interval. "error": "failed to make an HTTP request: Post \"http://backend:4318/v1/traces\": context deadline exceeded (Client.Timeout exceeded while awaiting headers)", "interval": "6.236881119s"

    The backend took the connection and did not answer within the exporter timeout (30s for otlphttp, 5s for the gRPC otlp exporter). The backend is overloaded or a proxy is holding the request. Raise timeout only after the backend is healthy. With retry_on_failure.enabled: false the same error ends in Exporting failed. Dropping data. Try enabling retry_on_failure config option to retry on retryable errors.

    Exporting failed. Rejecting data. "error": "sending queue is full", "rejected_items": 50

    The exporter's sending_queue is full because the backend is slower than the input, so new data is refused and the batch processor logs Sender failed. Over HTTP the sender sees a 503. Older releases word it Dropping data because sending_queue is full. Try increasing queue_size.

    Exporting failed. Dropping data. "error": "no more retries left: rpc error: code = Unavailable desc = error exporting items, request to http://backend:4318/v1/traces responded with HTTP Status Code 503", "dropped_items": 50

    The backend answered 503 on every retry until max_elapsed_time. Look at the backend's own health and logs. A 502 or 504 is retried the same way and usually points at a load balancer in front of the backend.

    Exporting failed. Will retry the request after interval. "error": "rpc error: code = ResourceExhausted desc = error exporting items, request to http://backend:4318/v1/traces responded with HTTP Status Code 429", "interval": "1.132719217s"

    The backend is rate limiting. The Collector retries with growing intervals until max_elapsed_time. If it keeps happening, lower the load (a batch processor sends fewer, larger requests) or raise the quota.

    "error": "no more retries left: rpc error: code = Unavailable desc = connection error: desc = \"transport: authentication handshake failed: tls: failed to verify certificate: x509: certificate signed by unknown authority\""

    The exporter uses TLS and does not trust the backend's certificate. Set tls.ca_file to the issuing CA. Do not use insecure_skip_verify outside a test. An expired certificate prints an x509: certificate has expired or is not yet valid error here instead.

    "error": "no more retries left: rpc error: code = Unavailable desc = connection error: desc = \"transport: authentication handshake failed: tls: first record does not look like a TLS handshake\""

    The exporter speaks TLS to a backend that speaks plain text: the port is not the TLS port, or the backend has no TLS. Use the right port, or set tls.insecure: true for a plain backend inside a trusted network.

    "error": "no more retries left: rpc error: code = Unavailable desc = connection error: desc = \"error reading server preface: EOF\""

    The exporter sends plain text (tls.insecure: true) to a backend that requires TLS and closed the connection. Remove insecure and set tls.ca_file. The same text appears when the port is not a gRPC server at all.

    "error": "failed to make an HTTP request: Post \"https://backend:4318/v1/traces\": http: server gave HTTP response to HTTPS client"

    The endpoint says https:// but the backend serves plain HTTP. Use http:// for a plain backend, or fix the port.

    "error": "failed to make an HTTP request: Post \"http://backend:9999/v1/traces\": dial tcp 192.0.2.10:9999: connect: connection refused"

    Nothing listens on that address and port, or a firewall rejects it. Check the endpoint port against the backend's OTLP port (4317 for gRPC, 4318 for HTTP), and test with nc -vz HOST PORT from the Collector host.

    warn memorylimiter@v0.165.0/memorylimiter.go:284 Memory usage is above hard limit. Forcing a GC. "cur_mem_mib": 27 warn memorylimiter@v0.165.0/memorylimiter.go:303 Memory usage is above soft limit. Refusing data. "cur_mem_mib": 24

    The memory_limiter processor sees more memory in use than limit_mib (hard) or limit_mib minus spike_limit_mib (soft). It forces a garbage collection, then refuses incoming data until memory drops. Receivers return 503 and count otelcol_receiver_refused_*.

    {"code":14, "message":"data refused due to high memory usage"}

    The reply a client gets when the memory limiter refuses its data: HTTP 503 here, gRPC code 14 (Unavailable) there. The sender should retry. Raise the memory limit, add a batch processor after the limiter, or cut the input. Set limit_mib below the container limit.

    Error: cannot start pipelines: failed to start "otlp" receiver: listen tcp 0.0.0.0:4317: bind: address already in use

    Another process, often a second Collector or an agent, owns the receiver port. Find it with ss -ltnp, then stop it or change the receiver's endpoint. Nothing starts: the Collector exits instead of running with a pipeline missing.

    Error: failed to create meter provider: binding address localhost:8888 for Prometheus exporter: listen tcp 127.0.0.1:8888: bind: address already in use

    The Collector's own metrics port is taken, commonly by a second Collector on the same host. Move it with service::telemetry::metrics::readers, a pull reader with a different port.

    Error: service::pipelines::traces: references processor "batch" which is not configured

    A pipeline names a component that has no definition under processors:, receivers: or exporters:. Add the definition or remove the name. The same shape covers a missing exporter or extension.

    'protocols.grpc' decoding failed due to the following error(s): 'configgrpc.ServerConfig' has invalid keys: endpont

    A key is misspelled or does not exist for that component or version. The error lists the bad key. Check the component's README for the current key names; options are renamed between releases.

    Error: exporters::otlphttp/backend::retry_on_failure: 'max_elapsed_time' must not be less than 'max_interval'

    The retry settings contradict each other. max_interval defaults to 30s, so a short max_elapsed_time fails validation. Set max_interval below max_elapsed_time, or max_elapsed_time: 0 to retry without a time limit.

    Error: exporters::otlp: requires a non-empty "endpoint" Error: invalid configuration: exporters::otlp/backend::clientconfig: address backend: missing port in address

    The first: an exporter without an endpoint, or one set from an unset ${env:VAR} that expands to an empty string. The second: a gRPC endpoint needs host:port. Both stop validate and the start.

    "otlp" alias is deprecated; use "otlp_grpc" instead "otlphttp" alias is deprecated; use "otlp_http" instead

    A warning that the component type has a new name. The config still works in 0.165, but a later release can drop the old name, so change otlp to otlp_grpc and otlphttp to otlp_http in the config before upgrading.

    415 unsupported media type, supported: [application/json, application/x-protobuf] {"code":3, "message":"ReadObject: expect \" after {, but found b, error found in #2 byte of ...|{bad|..., bigger context ...|{bad|..."}

    Replies from the OTLP HTTP receiver. A 415 is a wrong Content-Type; a 400 is a body that does not parse as OTLP. Sending HTTP to the gRPC port 4317 gives a reset connection instead, and GET on a signal path gives 405. Check the client's protocol setting.

    Message queues

    Brokers fail by backing up: a consumer stops, a replica drops out, a disk fills, and the queue grows until producers block. Read the depth and the consumers first, then the broker's own health. Kafka commands are the kafka-*.sh scripts in the bin/ folder of the Apache tarball (KRaft mode, Kafka 3.7 and 4.x); BROKER is a host:port that answers, and --command-config FILE carries TLS or SASL settings so no password sits on the command line. RabbitMQ commands run on the node (add -n rabbit@HOST for another) as the user that owns .erlang.cookie; output is from RabbitMQ 4.x.

    Kafka: cluster and controller

    kafka-metadata-quorum.sh --bootstrap-server BROKER describe --status

    The KRaft controller quorum. LeaderId is the active controller, CurrentVoters lists every voter, and MaxFollowerLag and MaxFollowerLagTimeMs should sit near 0. A lag that keeps growing means a voter is down or slow. With a majority of voters down the call itself times out.

    kafka-metadata-quorum.sh --bootstrap-server BROKER describe --replication

    One row per voter. Lag is how many records it is behind the leader; a stale LastFetchTimestamp and a rising Lag mark the dead voter. Status is Leader, Follower or Observer.

    kafka-broker-api-versions.sh --bootstrap-server BROKER | grep 'id:'

    One line per live broker, such as localhost:9092 (id: 1 rack: null) -> (; Kafka 4.0 adds isFenced: false. A broker missing from the list is down, unreachable or fenced. Add | wc -l to count brokers.

    kafka-features.sh --bootstrap-server BROKER describe

    FinalizedVersionLevel against SupportedMaxVersion for metadata.version and the other features. After an upgrade a lower finalized level means the new features are still off. Raise it only after every broker runs the new version.

    Kafka: partitions and replicas

    kafka-topics.sh --bootstrap-server BROKER --describe --under-replicated-partitions

    Empty output is healthy. Each line is a partition whose Isr list is shorter than Replicas: a follower is down, slow, or its disk is full. A broker log that keeps shrinking and expanding the ISR points to a slow follower (watch IsrShrinksPerSec and IsrExpandsPerSec). replica.lag.time.max.ms sets how long a follower may lag before it leaves.

    kafka-topics.sh --bootstrap-server BROKER --describe --under-min-isr-partitions

    Partitions with fewer in-sync replicas than min.insync.replicas. Producers using acks=all get NotEnoughReplicasException on these; reads still work. Bring the missing replica back before you lower min.insync.replicas.

    kafka-topics.sh --bootstrap-server BROKER --describe --unavailable-partitions

    Prints only partitions with no leader, because every replica is offline. Empty output means every partition has a leader. These partitions fail both reads and writes.

    kafka-topics.sh --bootstrap-server BROKER --describe --topic TOPIC

    Per partition: Leader, Replicas (the first is the preferred leader) and Isr. A Leader that differs from the first replica is a skew after a restart. Kafka 4.x also prints Elr and LastKnownElr columns, empty when healthy.

    kafka-leader-election.sh --bootstrap-server BROKER --election-type preferred --all-topic-partitions

    Hands leadership back to each partition's preferred replica, but only where the current leader is not already it. Clients reconnect for a moment. Run it once the stopped broker is back in the ISR.

    Changes state

    kafka-leader-election.sh --bootstrap-server BROKER --election-type unclean --topic TOPIC --partition 0

    Elects a leader that is not in the ISR, only for a partition that has no leader. It can lose acknowledged records and make replicas diverge. Last resort: the usual fix is to bring a broker with the data back.

    Can lose data

    kafka-topics.sh --bootstrap-server BROKER --list

    Topic names, including the internal __consumer_offsets. A topic you did not create is usually a client with auto-creation on: see auto.create.topics.enable.

    Kafka: consumer groups and lag

    kafka-consumer-groups.sh --bootstrap-server BROKER --list --state

    Every group with its state: Stable, PreparingRebalance, CompletingRebalance, Empty or Dead. Empty is a group with offsets and no live members. Groups on the 4.x consumer protocol (group.protocol=consumer) show Assigning or Reconciling instead of the two rebalance states.

    kafka-consumer-groups.sh --bootstrap-server BROKER --describe --group GROUPNAME

    Lag per partition is LOG-END-OFFSET minus CURRENT-OFFSET: a row with 100, 2822 and 2722 is a group 2722 records behind. A lag that grows while the group has members means slow consumers; a - in CONSUMER-ID and a first line saying "has no active members" mean nobody is consuming. One partition far above the rest is a hot key or one stuck consumer.

    kafka-consumer-groups.sh --bootstrap-server BROKER --describe --group GROUPNAME --state

    STATE, #MEMBERS, the COORDINATOR (ID) broker and the ASSIGNMENT-STRATEGY. Run it a few times: a group that keeps returning to PreparingRebalance (or Reconciling) is in a rebalance storm.

    kafka-consumer-groups.sh --bootstrap-server BROKER --describe --group GROUPNAME --members

    One row per member with CLIENT-ID, HOST and #PARTITIONS. A member with 0 partitions is idle because the group has more consumers than partitions. Members whose host you do not recognise are old deployments still running.

    kafka-consumer-groups.sh --bootstrap-server BROKER --describe --all-groups

    The lag table for every group. Fine for a quick look; on a cluster with hundreds of groups name the group instead, as it asks the coordinator for each one.

    kafka-get-offsets.sh --bootstrap-server BROKER --topic TOPIC

    Latest offset per partition, as topic:partition:offset. Run it twice, a minute apart, to get the produce rate; an offset that never moves means producers are stuck.

    Kafka: resetting offsets

    kafka-consumer-groups.sh --bootstrap-server BROKER --group GROUPNAME --topic TOPIC --reset-offsets --to-earliest --dry-run

    Prints the NEW-OFFSET for each partition and changes nothing; always run it before --execute. The group must be Empty, so stop every consumer first. Swap --to-earliest for --to-latest, --to-offset N or --shift-by -100; Kafka clamps a shift below the earliest offset to the earliest and warns.

    kafka-consumer-groups.sh --bootstrap-server BROKER --group GROUPNAME --topic TOPIC --reset-offsets --to-datetime 2026-01-01T00:00:00.000 --dry-run

    Plans a move to the first record at or after that time. An empty partition falls back to the latest offset and prints a Warn: line.

    kafka-consumer-groups.sh --bootstrap-server BROKER --group GROUPNAME --topic TOPIC --reset-offsets --to-earliest --execute

    Replays the whole topic for that group. Everything still on disk is processed again, so consumers must tolerate duplicates. Save each partition's CURRENT-OFFSET from --describe first: to undo, reset each partition back with --topic TOPIC:PARTITION --to-offset N (partitions after the colon, comma-separated), or use --from-file with a CSV.

    Changes state

    kafka-consumer-groups.sh --bootstrap-server BROKER --group GROUPNAME --topic TOPIC --reset-offsets --to-latest --execute

    Skips the backlog: the group never processes records between its old offset and the end. Use it only when dropping the backlog is acceptable, and save the current offsets from --describe first.

    Can lose data

    kafka-consumer-groups.sh --bootstrap-server BROKER --delete --group GROUPNAME

    Deletes the group and its committed offsets, so a consumer with the same group id starts from auto.offset.reset. The group must be Empty, or you get GroupNotEmptyException.

    Can lose data

    Kafka: disk, retention and logs

    kafka-log-dirs.sh --bootstrap-server BROKER --describe --topic-list TOPIC

    JSON per broker and log directory, with each partition's size in bytes and offsetLag for a replica that is catching up. A log directory whose error is not null (an exception class name) has gone offline after a disk error.

    df -h MNT; du -sh MNT/* | sort -h | tail -10

    Free space on the volume named by log.dirs, then the ten biggest partition folders (named topic-partition). A broker that runs out of space stops serving the log directory, so alert well before 90 percent.

    kafka-configs.sh --bootstrap-server BROKER --entity-type topics --entity-name TOPIC --describe --all | grep -E 'retention|segment|cleanup'

    The effective retention.ms, retention.bytes, segment.bytes and cleanup.policy, each with where it comes from in synonyms. Retention deletes only closed segments, so a quiet topic can keep data past its retention until the 1 GiB segment rolls (segment.ms forces it).

    kafka-configs.sh --bootstrap-server BROKER --entity-type topics --entity-name TOPIC --alter --add-config retention.ms=86400000

    Sets the topic's retention to one day; older closed segments go on the next retention check, which cannot be undone. Revert the setting with --alter --delete-config retention.ms.

    Can lose data

    [GroupCoordinator id=1 topic=__consumer_offsets partition=43] Preparing to rebalance group g2 in state PreparingRebalance with old generation 1 (reason: Adding new member c-b-be05c4a1 with group instance id null; ...

    A coordinator log line, one per rebalance, with the reason. Count them per minute: repeated lines for one group, each with a new reason, are a storm. Fix the cause (slow poll(), max.poll.interval.ms, flapping pods, session.timeout.ms too low), not the broker.

    Kafka errors

    org.apache.kafka.common.errors.RecordTooLargeException: The message is 2000088 bytes when serialized which is larger than 1048576, which is the value of the max.request.size configuration.

    The record is bigger than the producer's max.request.size. The broker has its own limit too: message.max.bytes, or max.message.bytes on the topic. Raise all of them together, and fetch.max.bytes and max.partition.fetch.bytes on consumers.

    org.apache.kafka.common.errors.NotEnoughReplicasException: Messages are rejected since there are fewer in-sync replicas than required.

    A write with acks=all found fewer in-sync replicas than min.insync.replicas. The client logs NOT_ENOUGH_REPLICAS and retries first. Check --under-min-isr-partitions and bring the missing broker back.

    org.apache.kafka.common.errors.NotLeaderOrFollowerException

    The client sent a request to a broker that no longer leads that partition. It is normal for a moment during a leader move or restart, and the client refreshes metadata. If it repeats, check advertised.listeners: two brokers advertising the same address (one load balancer, localhost) send requests to the wrong broker.

    org.apache.kafka.common.errors.TimeoutException: Topic nosuch not present in metadata after 60000 ms. org.apache.kafka.common.errors.TimeoutException: Expiring 1 record(s) for orders-0:120000 ms has passed since batch creation

    The first: the producer could not learn the topic's leader within max.block.ms, so the topic is missing or the cluster is unreachable. The second: a batch waited longer than delivery.timeout.ms, because the leader was down, slow or under min ISR. Check broker reachability first.

    Call(callName=describeMetadataQuorum, deadlineMs=1795191471869, tries=1, nextAllowedTryMs=1795191472033) timed out at 1795191471931 after 1 attempt(s)

    An admin tool call timed out, with DisconnectException ... due to node 1 being disconnected below it. Seen when two of three controllers were stopped: no quorum, no answers. Check every controller with docker ps or systemctl, then the controller port (9093 by default).

    [AdminClient clientId=adminclient-1] Connection to node -1 (localhost/127.0.0.1:9999) could not be established. Node may not be available.

    The tool cannot open a TCP connection to the bootstrap address, and repeats until its timeout. The address or port is wrong, the broker is down, or a firewall drops it. Check --bootstrap-server matches a listener, then nc -vz to it.

    org.apache.kafka.clients.consumer.CommitFailedException: Commit cannot be completed since the group has already rebalanced and assigned the partitions to another member. This means that the time between subsequent calls to poll() was longer than the configured max.poll.interval.ms ...

    The consumer took too long between poll() calls, so the group moved its partitions. Process less per poll (max.poll.records), speed up the handler, or raise max.poll.interval.ms. Expect duplicates for the uncommitted batch.

    org.apache.kafka.common.errors.RebalanceInProgressException: The group is rebalancing, so a rejoin is needed.

    The group is mid-rebalance, so commits and heartbeats are refused until members rejoin. One is normal after a deploy. If it keeps returning, see the rebalance storm entry: look for members failing their heartbeat.

    Error: Assignments can only be reset if the group 'g2' is inactive, but the current state is Stable.

    A reset needs an Empty group. Stop every consumer (or scale the deployment to 0), wait for --describe --state to show Empty, then run the reset again.

    Error while executing topic command : Unable to replicate the partition 3 time(s): The target replication factor of 3 cannot be reached because only 1 broker(s) are registered.

    InvalidReplicationFactorException: the topic asks for more replicas than there are live brokers. Lower the factor, or bring the missing brokers back. A broker that is down at creation time gives the same error.

    {nosuch-auto=UNKNOWN_TOPIC_OR_PARTITION}

    Seen in a producer WARN about a recoverable metadata issue: the topic does not exist yet. With auto.create.topics.enable=true (the apache/kafka default) the broker then creates it with default partitions, so a typo makes a new topic. Turn auto-creation off in production.

    Error while executing topic command : Topic 'orders' already exists.

    TopicExistsException. Run --describe --topic TOPIC to see its real settings, and use kafka-configs.sh --alter or kafka-topics.sh --alter --partitions N to change them. Partitions can only grow.

    Group 'nosuch' could not be deleted due to: org.apache.kafka.common.errors.GroupIdNotFoundException: The group id does not exist.

    The group has no committed offsets or was already deleted. Check the spelling with --list. Groups also disappear when offsets.retention.minutes (7 days by default) passes after the last member left.

    RabbitMQ: node and cluster health

    rabbitmq-diagnostics ping

    Only proves the Erlang VM is up and the CLI can talk to it. A node can pass this while the broker app is stopped, so follow with check_running.

    rabbitmq-diagnostics check_running

    Exits non-zero unless the RabbitMQ app is fully booted. After rabbitmqctl stop_app it prints "is not running or has not fully booted yet (check with is_booting)". Use it as the liveness probe for the broker itself, not ping.

    rabbitmq-diagnostics status

    Version, uptime, Erlang processes, file descriptors, listeners and memory. Read Memory high watermark setting and Low free disk space watermark, the two limits that trigger alarms, and compare Total memory used with the watermark.

    rabbitmq-diagnostics alarms

    Lists resource alarms in effect, for example "Memory alarm on node rabbit@rmq1" or "Free disk space alarm on node ...". "reported no alarms, local or clusterwide" means publishers are not blocked. check_local_alarms is deprecated and a no-op in 4.x.

    rabbitmq-diagnostics check_alarms

    Same check, with an exit code: 0 when clean, non-zero (69 seen) while any alarm is in effect. Put it in a monitoring probe.

    rabbitmq-diagnostics memory_breakdown --unit MB

    Where the node's memory goes, largest first. Look at queue_procs, binary (message bodies), connection_readers and connection_channels, quorum_ets, mgmt_db and other_proc. reserved_unallocated is memory the runtime holds but is not using.

    rabbitmq-diagnostics listeners

    Interface, port and protocol of each listener: 5672 for AMQP, 15672 for the management API, 25672 for clustering and CLI, 15692 for Prometheus. A missing line is a listener that failed to bind.

    rabbitmq-diagnostics check_port_connectivity

    Opens a TCP connection to every listener using the node's hostname. It catches a name that resolves to the wrong address, which listeners cannot. Pair it with check_virtual_hosts, which confirms every vhost is running.

    rabbitmq-diagnostics cluster_status

    Disk nodes, running nodes, versions, maintenance status, Alarms and Network Partitions. A node listed under disk nodes but not under running nodes is down. Any entry under partitions means a split that needs a decision.

    rabbitmq-diagnostics check_if_node_is_quorum_critical

    Before stopping a node, asks whether any quorum queue or stream would lose its majority. Non-zero means yes: wait for the other replicas to catch up. On a single node it says the check does not apply.

    rabbitmq-queues quorum_status QUEUE

    A table per replica: Raft State (leader or follower), Membership, Last Log Index, Last Written, Last Applied and Commit Index. A follower whose index trails the leader's by a lot is catching up or stuck. It fails on a classic queue.

    rabbitmq-diagnostics check_for_quorum_queues_without_an_elected_leader --across-all-vhosts '.*'

    Checks that every quorum queue matching the regular expression has an available leader. A non-zero exit lists the queues without one, usually because a majority of replicas is down. The pattern argument is required.

    RabbitMQ 4.0.8+

    rabbitmq-diagnostics check_if_any_deprecated_features_are_used

    Before a major upgrade: lists features the cluster still uses that the next series removes, such as classic queue mirroring. "Cluster reported no deprecated features in use" is a clean answer.

    rabbitmq-diagnostics check_certificate_expiration

    Checks every TLS certificate the node loads and warns about any expiring within the window (four weeks by default). "No certificates are expiring within 4 weeks." is clean.

    rabbitmq-diagnostics maybe_stuck

    Samples Erlang processes for ten seconds and lists suspicious ones. A healthy idle node can still report one (an idle system process), so act only if the stack belongs to a queue or channel. It is slow and noisy: use it when a queue hangs.

    RabbitMQ: queues, consumers and connections

    rabbitmqctl list_queues name type messages messages_ready messages_unacknowledged consumers memory state

    messages_ready is waiting for a consumer; messages_unacknowledged is delivered but not acked. Ready growing with consumers at 0 means nobody listens. Unacknowledged pinned at the prefetch count (10 here) means a consumer receives but never acks. state other than running needs a look.

    rabbitmqctl list_queues name consumer_utilisation message_bytes message_bytes_unacknowledged head_message_timestamp

    consumer_utilisation is the share of time a classic queue can deliver; far below 1 means consumers are slow or prefetch is too low. message_bytes shows size, not count. head_message_timestamp stays empty unless publishers set a timestamp.

    rabbitmqctl list_consumers

    Each consumer with its queue_name, channel_pid, ack_required, prefetch_count and active. A queue missing here has no consumer. Several consumers on one queue with active false are standby (single active consumer).

    rabbitmqctl list_channels connection name consumer_count messages_unacknowledged prefetch_count

    Per channel: how many unacknowledged messages it holds against its prefetch_count. One channel with a large unacked count is the stuck consumer. Thousands of channels on one connection is a client leak.

    rabbitmqctl list_connections name user state channels peer_host peer_port connected_at

    state is running, blocking or blocked. blocked is a publisher the broker has stopped because of an alarm; blocking has not published yet. connected_at is epoch milliseconds: many very recent values is churn. user shows who owns a connection.

    closing AMQP connection ([::1]:33690 -> [::1]:5672, duration: '2ms')

    A broker log line for every closed connection. Many per second with short duration values means a client opens a connection for each message. It wastes CPU and file descriptors. Reuse one long-lived connection and a channel per thread.

    curl -s --netrc-file ~/.rabbitmq.netrc 'http://HOST:15672/api/nodes?columns=name,mem_used,mem_limit,mem_alarm,disk_free,disk_free_limit,disk_free_alarm,fd_used,fd_total'

    Limits and use in one JSON line per node, from the management API. Put machine HOST, login and password lines in ~/.rabbitmq.netrc (mode 600) so the password never appears in a command. fd_used near fd_total is a descriptor leak. These figures refresh every few seconds.

    curl -s -o /dev/null -w '%{http_code}\n' --netrc-file ~/.rabbitmq.netrc 'http://HOST:15672/api/health/checks/alarms'

    200 means no alarm in the cluster; 503 means at least one, and the body names the node and resource (disk space or memory). Good for a load balancer or Kubernetes probe. Use HTTPS on port 15671 when TLS is on.

    rabbitmqctl purge_queue QUEUE

    Deletes every ready message in the queue; unacknowledged ones stay with their consumers. It cannot be undone. Use it for a poison backlog you have already saved or decided to drop. A missing queue prints "Object (queue, stream, exchange, etc) was not found".

    Can lose data

    rabbitmqctl stop_app && rabbitmqctl start_app

    Restarts the RabbitMQ app inside the same Erlang VM, closing every client connection on that node. Non-persistent messages in classic queues are lost. Use it to clear a stuck broker without rebooting. In a cluster check check_if_node_is_quorum_critical first.

    Can lose data

    RabbitMQ: alarms that block publishers

    rabbitmqctl set_vm_memory_high_watermark 0.5

    Sets the memory alarm threshold to half of RAM for this run only; a restart restores the config value. When used memory passes it, the broker blocks every publishing connection until it falls back. Raising it buys time but leaves less headroom for the OS: fix what holds memory (memory_breakdown, long queues) instead.

    Changes state

    rabbitmqctl list_connections name state

    During a memory or disk alarm, publishers show blocked and idle connections blocking, as seen with a 50 MB threshold. Consumers keep working, so draining the queues is what clears the alarm. Clients should handle the connection.blocked notification.

    rabbitmqctl set_disk_free_limit 2GB

    Changes the free-disk threshold for the running node (default 50MB, a restart restores the config value). Below it the broker raises a "Free disk space alarm" and blocks publishers. Set it to at least the size of RAM if the node can page messages to disk; free space or grow the volume.

    Changes state

    RabbitMQ errors

    Error when reading /var/lib/rabbitmq/.erlang.cookie: eacces

    The Erlang VM cannot read its cookie file, so the node will not start. The file must be owned by the user that runs RabbitMQ with mode 400 or 600. Fix with chown and chmod. The same cookie must exist for CLI tools and cluster peers.

    Error: unable to perform an operation on node 'rabbit@rmq1'. Please see diagnostics information and suggestions below.

    The CLI could not reach the node. Causes, in order: the node is down, the name or hostname differs from -n, or the CLI's cookie does not match the node's. The diagnostics below the error say which; start with rabbitmq-diagnostics ping.

    ProbableAuthenticationError ConnectionClosedByBroker: (403) 'ACCESS_REFUSED - Login was refused using authentication mechanism PLAIN. For details see the broker logfile.'

    Wrong user or password, or an auth backend refusal. The broker log has the reason: PLAIN login refused: user 'probe' - invalid credentials. The guest user can connect only from localhost. Test with rabbitmqctl authenticate_user and the user name only: it prompts for the password, which then stays out of the process list.

    NOT_ALLOWED - vhost nope not found

    The client named a virtual host that does not exist (it arrives as a 530 connection close). Check rabbitmqctl list_vhosts and the client's vhost setting. A vhost the user has no permission on gives access to vhost ... refused instead.

    PRECONDITION_FAILED - inequivalent arg 'durable' for queue 'q1' in vhost '/': received 'false' but current is 'true'

    The client redeclared a queue with different settings. RabbitMQ never changes a queue's arguments in place. Match the declaration, or delete the queue and declare it again once you have drained it. Policies change x- settings without a redeclare.

    NOT_FOUND - no queue 'missing-q' in vhost '/'

    A consume or a passive declare named a queue that does not exist. A typo, a wrong vhost, or a queue deleted by an expiry or auto-delete rule. Check rabbitmqctl list_queues -p with the vhost the client uses.

    PRECONDITION_FAILED - delivery acknowledgement on channel 1 timed out. Timeout value used: 1800000 ms. This timeout value can be configured, see consumers doc guide

    A consumer held a delivery for longer than consumer_timeout (30 minutes by default) without acking, so the broker closed the channel. Ack sooner, move slow work off the delivery path, or raise consumer_timeout. The message returns to the queue.

    missed heartbeats from client, timeout: 60s

    Log reason for a closed connection: the client sent no frame for two heartbeat intervals. The client is blocked (a long GC, a busy single thread) or a firewall or load balancer dropped the idle connection. Shorten the heartbeat below the network device's idle timeout.

    {:unsupported, :rabbit_classic_queue}

    You ran a quorum-only command, such as rabbitmq-queues quorum_status, against a classic queue. Check the queue's type in list_queues name type.

    Error: No such queue was found

    From rabbitmqctl delete_queue when the name is wrong or the vhost is not the default one. Add -p and the vhost name for another vhost.

    PostgreSQL

    Most incidents are a lock, a long transaction, connection exhaustion, a full disk or a replica that fell behind. pg_stat_activity shows the first three; the replication and WAL groups show the rest.

    Connect

    pg_isready -h HOST -p 5432

    Exit 0: accepting. 1: rejecting, usually still starting up. 2: no response.

    psql "host=HOST dbname=DB user=DBUSER sslmode=require"

    Then \conninfo, \l, \dt+, \d+ TABLENAME, \x auto, \timing on. Keep the password in ~/.pgpass (mode 0600), not on the command line.

    SELECT version(), pg_postmaster_start_time(), pg_is_in_recovery(); SHOW data_directory;

    Which server you are on, since when, and whether it is a standby (t). A start time of a few minutes ago means a restart or a crash loop: read the server log next.

    SHOW hba_file; SELECT pg_reload_conf();

    Where the auth rules live. A reload applies pg_hba.conf and most settings; listen_addresses, max_connections and shared_buffers need a restart.

    Changes state

    journalctl -u 'postgresql*' -n 100

    The server log, when it goes to the journal. With logging_collector on (the RHEL and PGDG RPM default) the journal holds only start and stop, and psql -c 'SELECT pg_current_logfile()' names the real file, under DATADIR/log/. On Debian and Ubuntu it is under /var/log/postgresql/ instead. In the official Docker image it goes to docker logs CTR.

    What is running

    SELECT pid, usename, state, wait_event_type, wait_event, now() - xact_start AS xact_age, left(query, 80) AS query FROM pg_stat_activity WHERE state <> 'idle' ORDER BY xact_age DESC NULLS LAST;

    Everything not idle, oldest transaction first. "idle in transaction" with a large age holds locks and stops vacuum; set idle_in_transaction_session_timeout.

    SELECT wait_event_type, wait_event, count(*) FROM pg_stat_activity WHERE state = 'active' GROUP BY 1, 2 ORDER BY 3 DESC;

    Where active sessions spend their time. Lock means they queue behind a lock, IO means reads or writes, LWLock means internal contention, Client means the app is slow to read results. An empty type is plain CPU.

    SELECT pid, usename, application_name, client_addr, state, now() - query_start AS running_for, left(query, 80) AS query FROM pg_stat_activity WHERE state = 'active' AND backend_type = 'client backend' AND now() - query_start > interval '30 seconds' ORDER BY running_for DESC;

    Queries running longer than 30 seconds, with the app and client to blame. Check wait_event_type before cancelling: a query waiting on a Lock needs the blocker found, not killed.

    SELECT pid, pg_blocking_pids(pid) AS blocked_by, wait_event_type, left(query, 60) AS query FROM pg_stat_activity WHERE cardinality(pg_blocking_pids(pid)) > 0;

    Who waits on whom. Follow blocked_by to the root blocker, usually a long or idle-in-transaction session.

    SELECT pg_cancel_backend(PID);

    Cancels the running query. The session stays. Try this before the next entry.

    Changes state

    SELECT pg_terminate_backend(PID);

    Ends the session and rolls back its transaction. The client sees "terminating connection due to administrator command" and must reconnect.

    Changes state

    SET lock_timeout = '5s';

    Run it before ALTER TABLE in a migration. DDL waiting for a lock queues every later query behind it; this makes it give up instead.

    Connections and pooling

    SELECT backend_type, state, count(*) FROM pg_stat_activity GROUP BY 1, 2 ORDER BY 3 DESC; SHOW max_connections;

    Connections by type and state, against the limit. Only client backend rows count against max_connections; walsenders and background workers have their own slots.

    SELECT count(*) AS total, count(*) FILTER (WHERE state = 'active') AS active, count(*) FILTER (WHERE state LIKE 'idle in transaction%') AS idle_tx, current_setting('max_connections')::int AS max, current_setting('superuser_reserved_connections')::int AS reserved FROM pg_stat_activity WHERE backend_type = 'client backend';

    Headroom at a glance. Total near max minus reserved and most of it idle: the app pools are oversized. Many idle_tx: sessions that begin and never commit.

    SELECT usename, datname, application_name, client_addr, count(*) FROM pg_stat_activity WHERE backend_type = 'client backend' GROUP BY 1, 2, 3, 4 ORDER BY 5 DESC;

    Which app host or role uses the slots. One client address with hundreds of connections is a pool without a cap or a connection leak.

    SELECT rolname, rolconnlimit FROM pg_roles WHERE rolconnlimit <> -1; SELECT datname, datconnlimit FROM pg_database WHERE datconnlimit <> -1;

    Per-role and per-database caps. They produce "too many connections for role" and "too many connections for database" long before max_connections is reached.

    psql -h HOST -p 6432 -U DBUSER pgbouncer -c 'SHOW POOLS;'

    PgBouncer's admin console (the database named pgbouncer, the user must be in admin_users or stats_users). cl_waiting above 0 means clients queue for a server connection; maxwait is the longest wait in seconds. sv_active at the pool size with waiting clients: raise default_pool_size, or find the slow queries holding the servers. Columns shown are from PgBouncer 1.26.

    psql -h HOST -p 6432 -U DBUSER pgbouncer -c 'SHOW CLIENTS;' -c 'SHOW SERVERS;'

    One row per client and per server connection. A client with state waiting has link empty and a growing wait; an active one points by link to the server connection it uses, whose remote_pid is the pid in pg_stat_activity. Use it to find which app host holds the pool.

    psql -h HOST -p 6432 -U DBUSER pgbouncer -c 'SHOW STATS;' -c 'SHOW DATABASES;' -c 'SHOW LISTS;' -c 'SHOW CONFIG;'

    total_wait_time and avg_wait_time (microseconds) show queueing per database. SHOW DATABASES gives the real target, pool size and paused or disabled flags. SHOW LISTS counts clients and servers in use. SHOW CONFIG prints the live setting, so you can tell a stale reload.

    psql -h HOST -p 6432 -U DBUSER pgbouncer -c 'PAUSE DB;'

    Waits for in-flight transactions to finish, then holds new ones, so you can restart the backend. In session pooling it waits for every client to disconnect. Every app on that database waits until RESUME DB;.

    Changes state

    psql -h HOST -p 6432 -U DBUSER pgbouncer -c 'RESUME DB;'

    Lets the held clients through after a PAUSE. Paused with clients still piling up: run this first.

    Changes state

    psql -h HOST -p 6432 -U DBUSER pgbouncer -c 'RELOAD;'

    Re-reads pgbouncer.ini and the auth file; open connections stay.

    Changes state

    psql -h HOST -p 6432 -U DBUSER pgbouncer -c 'KILL DB;'

    Drops every client and server connection of that database at once. Applications see broken connections and must reconnect. Prefer PAUSE and RESUME.

    Can lose data

    Locks and deadlocks

    SELECT a.pid, a.usename, a.state, a.wait_event_type, a.wait_event, l.locktype, l.mode, l.granted, l.relation::regclass AS rel, left(a.query, 60) AS query FROM pg_locks l JOIN pg_stat_activity a USING (pid) WHERE NOT l.granted OR l.locktype = 'transactionid' ORDER BY a.pid;

    Held and waited locks side by side. A row with granted false is a waiter. A row-lock wait shows as a transactionid lock in ShareLock mode, not a lock on the row: the waiter sits behind the transaction that owns that id. A waiting relation lock is DDL, an explicit LOCK, or an ordinary query queued behind one.

    SELECT l.pid, l.mode, l.relation::regclass AS rel, a.state, now() - a.xact_start AS xact_age, left(a.query, 60) AS query FROM pg_locks l JOIN pg_stat_activity a USING (pid) WHERE l.locktype = 'relation' AND l.relation = 'TABLENAME'::regclass ORDER BY l.granted DESC, a.xact_start;

    Every session holding or waiting for a lock on one table, holders first. AccessExclusiveLock from ALTER TABLE, VACUUM FULL or LOCK blocks every reader behind it; a waiting AccessExclusiveLock also blocks all later queries.

    SELECT locktype, classid, objid, mode, granted, pid FROM pg_locks WHERE locktype = 'advisory';

    Application-level advisory locks. Look for a pid that holds one while the queue of waiters grows; the app, not the schema, is the cause.

    SHOW log_lock_waits; SHOW deadlock_timeout;

    With log_lock_waits on, any wait longer than deadlock_timeout (default 1s) is logged, and the deadlock check runs at the same moment. Set it to on in production: it is the only record of a lock wait after it ends.

    LOG: process 332 still waiting for ShareLock on transaction 756 after 1000.083 ms DETAIL: Process holding the lock: 325. Wait queue: 332. CONTEXT: while updating tuple (0,1) in relation "t"

    A row-lock wait. The holder is process 325; find it with pg_stat_activity. When the wait ends, a second line says "acquired ShareLock on transaction 756 after 4999.284 ms". The STATEMENT line shows the waiter's query.

    ERROR: deadlock detected DETAIL: Process 360 waits for ShareLock on transaction 759; blocked by process 367. Process 367 waits for ShareLock on transaction 758; blocked by process 360. Process 360: update t set v='d1' where id=11 Process 367: update t set v='d2' where id=10 HINT: See server log for query details.

    The server log names both queries and processes. PostgreSQL aborted one transaction (here 360) to break the cycle. Fix the application to touch rows in one fixed order, and retry the aborted transaction. pg_stat_database.deadlocks counts them.

    SELECT datname, deadlocks, conflicts, temp_files, pg_size_pretty(temp_bytes) AS temp_bytes FROM pg_stat_database WHERE datname = 'DB';

    Deadlocks and temp-file spills since the last stats reset. deadlocks rising points at the lock-order bug above; many temp_files mean sorts and hashes spill because work_mem is too small.

    SET lock_timeout = '2s'; SET statement_timeout = '30s';

    Per-session limits for a migration or a script. lock_timeout ends the wait with "canceling statement due to lock timeout"; statement_timeout ends a running query with "canceling statement due to statement timeout". Put them in the role with ALTER ROLE ... SET for apps.

    Long transactions and the xmin horizon

    SELECT pid, usename, application_name, state, now() - xact_start AS xact_age, greatest(age(backend_xid), age(backend_xmin)) AS xmin_age, left(query, 60) AS query FROM pg_stat_activity WHERE backend_xid IS NOT NULL OR backend_xmin IS NOT NULL ORDER BY greatest(age(backend_xid), age(backend_xmin)) DESC NULLS LAST;

    Sessions pinning old row versions. Vacuum cannot remove a row that any of them might still see. The top row is your horizon holder; "idle in transaction" in state is the usual culprit. xmin_age counts transactions, not time.

    SELECT 'slot' AS holder, slot_name::text AS name, xmin, catalog_xmin, greatest(age(xmin), age(catalog_xmin)) AS xmin_age FROM pg_replication_slots WHERE xmin IS NOT NULL OR catalog_xmin IS NOT NULL UNION ALL SELECT 'prepared xact', gid, transaction, NULL, age(transaction) FROM pg_prepared_xacts UNION ALL SELECT 'standby feedback', application_name, backend_xmin, NULL, age(backend_xmin) FROM pg_stat_replication WHERE backend_xmin IS NOT NULL;

    The other things that hold the horizon besides sessions: replication slots (a logical slot holds catalog_xmin), forgotten prepared transactions, and standbys with hot_standby_feedback on. With a slot, the standby's feedback shows in the slot's xmin, not in pg_stat_replication.

    SELECT gid, prepared, owner, database, age(transaction) AS xid_age FROM pg_prepared_xacts;

    Two-phase transactions left by a crashed coordinator. They survive restarts, keep locks and hold back vacuum. Needs max_prepared_transactions above 0 (default 0). Check prepared for old rows.

    ROLLBACK PREPARED 'GID';

    Rolls back a prepared transaction by its gid; use COMMIT PREPARED 'GID' instead if the coordinator had decided to commit. Connect to the database in the database column first, or it fails with "prepared transaction belongs to another database". Ask the owning app first: a wrong choice loses or duplicates work.

    Can lose data

    removable cutoff: 772, which was 1 XIDs old when operation ended tuples: 0 removed, 86642 remain, 0 are dead but not yet removable

    From VACUUM (VERBOSE) or an autovacuum log line. The removable cutoff is the xmin horizon vacuum used. If it is far behind the current transaction id, something above holds it. Dead rows "not yet removable" are the ones kept alive by it. PostgreSQL 14 has no cutoff line: the horizon is the oldest xmin at the end of the tuples line.

    PostgreSQL 15+

    SET idle_in_transaction_session_timeout = '60s';

    Ends sessions that sit "idle in transaction" longer than that, with "terminating connection due to idle-in-transaction timeout". Set it per role for apps. Never set it for a role that runs long manual work.

    Vacuum and bloat

    SELECT relname, n_live_tup, n_dead_tup, round(100.0 * n_dead_tup / nullif(n_live_tup + n_dead_tup, 0), 1) AS dead_pct, last_autovacuum, last_autoanalyze, autovacuum_count FROM pg_stat_user_tables ORDER BY n_dead_tup DESC LIMIT 10;

    Tables with the most dead rows. A high dead_pct with an old or empty last_autovacuum: autovacuum is not reaching the table. Check the horizon, autovacuum_enabled in the table options, and the workers.

    SELECT pid, datname, relid::regclass AS tbl, phase, round(100.0 * heap_blks_scanned / nullif(heap_blks_total, 0), 1) AS pct_scanned, index_vacuum_count FROM pg_stat_progress_vacuum;

    A running vacuum or autovacuum. index_vacuum_count above 1 means the dead-row list overflowed maintenance_work_mem and vacuum rescans the indexes; raise it. A wait event of VacuumDelay in pg_stat_activity is the cost throttle.

    SELECT name, setting, unit FROM pg_settings WHERE name LIKE 'autovacuum%' OR name LIKE 'vacuum_%' ORDER BY name;

    The thresholds that decide when autovacuum fires: autovacuum_vacuum_scale_factor 0.2 means after 20 percent of the table changed, which is far too late on a huge table. Cost settings decide how fast it runs; the default throttle is gentle.

    SELECT relname, reloptions FROM pg_class WHERE reloptions IS NOT NULL AND relkind = 'r';

    Per-table autovacuum overrides, including autovacuum_enabled=false left over from a bulk load. Tune a hot table with ALTER TABLE ... SET (autovacuum_vacuum_scale_factor = 0.01).

    VACUUM (VERBOSE, ANALYZE) TABLENAME;

    Vacuums and analyzes one table in your session without blocking reads or writes, except that the final truncation briefly takes ACCESS EXCLUSIVE; VACUUM (TRUNCATE false, VERBOSE, ANALYZE) avoids it. The verbose output shows pages removed, dead rows "not yet removable", and the cutoff. It returns space to the OS only from the end of the file.

    Changes state

    LOG: automatic vacuum of table "app.public.av1": index scans: 0 pages: 34 removed, 222 remain, 256 scanned (100.00% of total) tuples: 16666 removed, 33334 remain, 0 are dead but not yet removable

    Autovacuum finished one table; this needs log_autovacuum_min_duration set (0 logs all). Removed tuples show the work; many "dead but not yet removable" show a horizon holder. Long elapsed and many index scans: raise maintenance_work_mem. PostgreSQL 14 prints 0 skipped due to pins, 0 skipped frozen in place of the scanned count, and adds oldest xmin to the tuples line.

    ERROR: canceling autovacuum task

    A lock request (DDL, LOCK TABLE) conflicted with autovacuum, which backs off. If it repeats on the same table, autovacuum never finishes there. Schedule the DDL differently, and vacuum by hand in a quiet period.

    CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS pgstattuple; SELECT table_len, dead_tuple_percent, approx_free_percent FROM pgstattuple_approx('TABLENAME');

    A bloat estimate that skips the all-visible pages and reads the rest (pgstattuple() is exact but reads it all). dead_tuple_percent is rows vacuum can reclaim; approx_free_percent is space inside the file that stays allocated and is reusable. Contrib module; the extension needs superuser to create.

    Changes state

    SELECT index_size, avg_leaf_density, leaf_fragmentation FROM pgstatindex('INDEXNAME');

    A btree's health. It reads the whole index, so it is slow on a large one. avg_leaf_density far below 90 (the fill factor default) with a large index_size is a bloated index; leaf_fragmentation above about 30 hurts range scans. Fix with REINDEX INDEX CONCURRENTLY INDEXNAME;, which needs the extra disk space of a second copy.

    VACUUM FULL TABLENAME;

    Rewrites the table and returns space to the OS, but takes an ACCESS EXCLUSIVE lock for the whole run, so reads and writes queue, and it needs free disk equal to the table. Prefer a normal vacuum, or pg_repack (an extension, not in core) for online work.

    Can lose data

    Transaction ID wraparound

    SELECT datname, age(datfrozenxid) AS xid_age, mxid_age(datminmxid) AS mxid_age, round(100.0 * age(datfrozenxid) / current_setting('autovacuum_freeze_max_age')::int) AS pct_of_freeze_max FROM pg_database ORDER BY 2 DESC;

    How close each database is to a forced vacuum. At 100 percent of autovacuum_freeze_max_age (default 200 million) autovacuum starts an aggressive anti-wraparound pass, even if autovacuum is off. A limit near 2 billion stops writes; mxid_age follows the same logic for multixacts.

    SELECT c.oid::regclass AS tbl, age(c.relfrozenxid) AS xid_age, pg_size_pretty(pg_total_relation_size(c.oid)) AS size FROM pg_class c JOIN pg_namespace n ON n.oid = c.relnamespace WHERE c.relkind IN ('r', 'm', 't') ORDER BY age(c.relfrozenxid) DESC LIMIT 10;

    The tables that hold datfrozenxid back. Run VACUUM (FREEZE, VERBOSE) on the top ones, after the horizon holders in the group above are gone, or the freeze cannot advance.

    WARNING: database "template1" must be vacuumed within 3484377 transactions HINT: To avoid transaction ID assignment failures, execute a database-wide VACUUM in that database. You might also need to commit or roll back old prepared transactions, or drop stale replication slots.

    Fewer than about 40 million transactions remain before PostgreSQL refuses new transaction ids, and writes stop when the number reaches 3 million, not 0. Act now: remove horizon holders (prepared transactions, slots, old sessions), then run VACUUM in the named database. PostgreSQL 14 to 16 word the hint "To avoid a database shutdown".
